My mom still has some of my old report cards, like from around fourth grade. They always said something like I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to actually finish my classwork and turn in my homework. \n\nI remember I would understand the lessons fine, but I'd just miss chunks of the instructions. Unless the teacher was standing right there keeping me on track, I'd just lose focus and not finish what I was supposed to be doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were in school, did you have particular trouble with reading, writing, or math specifically—like did you struggle to understand those subjects themselves—or was it more that you could understand the material but just had trouble staying focused and finishing the work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Unless the teacher was standing right there keeping me on track, I'd just lose focus and not finish what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did you have particular trouble with reading, writing, or math specifically—like did you struggle to understand those subjects themselves—or was it more that you could understand the material but just had trouble staying focused and finishing the work?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble understanding the subjects themselves. Like I said, I usually understood the lessons just fine. It was never really about not getting the math or struggling to read the words. It was definitely more about just... staying focused long enough to actually get the work done. I just couldn't keep my attention on it to finish the assignments.\n\nDoctor: When you had to write things down in school—like essays, written answers on tests, or written homework assignments—did you find the actual writing process itself difficult, or was it mainly that you didn't finish writing what you were supposed to write?\n\nPatient: It was definitely just not finishing. The writing itself wasn't the problem. I could write fine, and I knew what I wanted to say, but... getting it all down on paper before I lost focus was the hard part. I'd just trail off or get distracted, and the essay or whatever just wouldn't get done.\n\nDoctor: Back in school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you read more slowly than other kids, or that you had trouble understanding what you were reading—even when you were able to stay focused on it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone ever said that. I don't watch a lot of TV, and when I'm just hanging out at home or talking to my family, things seem pretty normal. It really just feels like a problem when I'm at the plant trying to get through my tasks.\n\nDoctor: When you're at the meat processing plant, have you noticed any exposure to strong smells, fumes, or loud noises that might be affecting you—and do you think the concentration problems might be related to those work conditions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's anything like that. I mean, the plant is loud and it definitely smells, but they give us earplugs and I'm pretty used to the environment by now. It's not like a fume or a noise distracts me and makes me lose my place. It just... happens.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing simpler, one-step tasks at work—like just moving items from one place to another, or checking a single thing—do you have the same trouble concentrating and finishing, or does it mainly happen with the longer, multi-step processes?\n\nPatient: It definitely happens more with the longer stuff. Like, if it's just moving things around, I can usually get through it fine. Unless the teacher was standing right there keeping me on track, I'd just lose focus and not finish what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did you have particular trouble with reading, writing, or math specifically—like did you struggle to understand those subjects themselves—or was it more that you could understand the material but just had trouble staying focused and finishing the work?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble understanding the subjects themselves. It was more just... staring out the window or doodling instead of paying attention, rather than needing to run around or fidget all the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're doing these multi-step tasks at work, is it specifically the last few steps that you tend to skip or leave incomplete—or do you sometimes lose focus and miss steps throughout the middle of the task as well?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don't watch a lot of TV, and when I'm just hanging out at home or talking to my family, things seem pretty normal. It really just feels like a problem when I'm at the plant trying to get through my tasks.\n\nDoctor: When you're at the meat processing plant, have you noticed any exposure to strong smells, fumes, or loud noises that might be affecting you—and do you think the concentration problems might be related to those work conditions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's anything like that. I mean, the plant is loud and it definitely smells, but they give us earplugs and I'm pretty used to the environment by now. It's not like a fume or a noise distracts me and makes me lose my place. It just... happens.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing simpler, one-step tasks at work—like just moving items from one place to another, or checking a single thing—do you have the same trouble concentrating and finishing, or does it mainly happen with the longer, multi-step processes?\n\nPatient: It definitely happens more with the longer stuff. Like, if it's just moving things around, I can usually get through it fine. But if it's something that takes a lot of focus for a while... it's really hard. \n\nLike, we have these certification modules and long procedure manuals we have to read, and I... I just put them off until the absolute last possible minute. Sitting there and trying to force my brain to absorb all those rules and steps, it honestly feels almost physically exhausting. I just try to avoid it as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did you have similar problems with finishing assignments, reading long materials, or staying focused on tasks that required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was actually an issue back then, too. Like I said, I usually understood the lessons just fine. It was never really about not getting the math or struggling to read the words. It was definitely more about just... staying focused long enough to actually get the work done. I just couldn't keep my attention on it to finish the assignments.\n\nDoctor: When you had to write things down in school—like essays, written answers on tests, or written homework assignments—did you find the actual writing process itself difficult, or was it mainly that you didn't finish writing what you were supposed to write?\n\nPatient: It was definitely just not finishing. And it's a big problem because I know my job absolutely depends on finishing those steps correctly, but I just keep missing the end of the process.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these tasks at work, are you able to concentrate and focus on what you're doing, or do you feel like you need to stop or move away from the task before you finish it?\n\nPatient: It's really hard to keep my focus. So it's not always that I physically walk away from the station, it's just that my brain kind of checks out and I can't concentrate on it anymore, no matter how much I want to.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, aside from work, have you noticed that you're having trouble concentrating or focusing on other things too—like at home, watching TV, or conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed it being an issue outside of work, honestly. I don't watch a lot of TV, and when I'm just hanging out at home or talking to my family, things seem pretty normal. It really just feels like a problem when I'm at the plant trying to get through my tasks.\n\nDoctor: When you're at the meat processing plant, have you noticed any exposure to strong smells, fumes, or loud noises that might be affecting you—and do you think the concentration problems might be related to those work conditions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's anything like that. I mean, the plant is loud and it definitely smells, but they give us earplugs and I'm pretty used to the environment by now. It's not like a fume or a noise distracts me and makes me lose my place. It just... happens.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing simpler, one-step tasks at work—like just moving items from one place to another, or checking a single thing—do you have the same trouble concentrating and finishing, or does it mainly happen with the longer, multi-step processes?\n\nPatient: It definitely happens more with the longer stuff. Like, if it's just moving things around, I can usually get through it fine. But if it's something that takes a lot of focus for a while... it's really hard. \n\nLike, we have these certification modules and long procedure manuals we have to read, and I... I just put them off until the absolute last possible minute. Sitting there and trying to force my brain to absorb all those rules and steps, it honestly feels almost physically exhausting. I just try to avoid it as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did you have similar problems with finishing assignments, reading long materials, or staying focused on tasks that required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was actually an issue back then, too. My mom still has some of my old report cards, like from around fourth grade. They always said something like I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to actually finish my classwork and turn in my homework. \n\nI remember I would understand the lessons fine, but I'd just miss chunks of the instructions. Unless the teacher was standing right there keeping me on track, I'd just lose focus and not finish what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did you have particular trouble with reading, writing, or math specifically—like did you struggle to understand those subjects themselves—or was it more that you could understand the material but just had trouble staying focused and finishing the work?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble understanding the subjects themselves. Like I said, I usually understood the lessons just fine. It was never really about not getting the math or struggling to read the words. It was definitely more about just... staying focused long enough to actually get the work done. I just couldn't keep my attention on it to finish the assignments.\n\nDoctor: When you had to write things down in school—like essays, written answers on tests, or written homework assignments—did you find the actual writing process itself difficult, or was it mainly that you didn't finish writing what you were supposed to write?\n\nPatient: It was definitely just not finishing. The writing itself wasn't the problem. I could write fine, and I knew what I wanted to say, but... getting it all down on paper before I lost focus was the hard part. I'd just trail off or get distracted, and the essay or whatever just wouldn't get done.\n\nDoctor: Back in school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you read more slowly than other kids, or that you had trouble understanding what you were reading—even when you were able to stay focused on it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone ever said that. I could always read at a normal pace, and I understood the words perfectly fine. It was really just about keeping my attention on the page. If I could actually stay focused, I understood what I was reading without any problems.\n\nDoctor: Right now, in your daily life outside of work, when you're doing something that requires you to concentrate for a while—like reading a book, watching a movie, or having a longer conversation—do you find yourself losing focus or having your mind wander, or does that seem to work okay for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely happens outside of work too. I'll be trying to read or watch something, and my mind just drifts off. But honestly, it's the worst at work. I'll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or log sheet for a while without actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. It just gets really hard to keep that concentration going.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did you ever get extra help, tutoring, or special services for reading, writing, or math—or did teachers ever suggest that you might have a learning disability in any of those areas?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't get any extra help like that. I never had tutors or anything, and no one ever brought up learning disabilities. The teachers just thought I wasn't trying hard enough or that I was daydreaming too much, but they never thought it was because I couldn't understand the material.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like a hobby, a game, or something you're genuinely excited about—do you still have trouble keeping your focus and finishing it, or does your attention seem to work better when you're doing something you like?\n\nPatient: No, if it's something I really like, my attention is actually fine. I understand the whole sequence perfectly. The problem is just... I keep starting the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and somehow I just leave out the last parts. It's like I just lose focus before I get to the end, even though I know exactly what I'm supposed to do and I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. I just can't seem to follow through and complete the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: When you were a kid in school, did teachers or your parents ever say you were hyperactive, restless, or that you had trouble sitting still—or did you tend to fidget a lot or feel like you needed to move around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't the kid bouncing off the walls or anything. Teachers never said I was hyperactive or restless. I usually sat pretty quietly, honestly. I know I definitely leave off the last parts a lot, like I mentioned, but... I haven't really paid close attention to whether I'm skipping things right in the middle too. It's just that when the paperwork or the logs are checked, parts are incomplete. So I might be losing track halfway through as well, I just haven't noticed exactly where my brain starts dropping the steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing one of these multi-step tasks and you realize it's incomplete, do you typically find that you've skipped steps scattered throughout—like maybe missing something from the beginning, middle, and end—or do you mostly find that the beginning and middle parts are done but the final steps are left off?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm just not sure. Like I said, I haven't really looked that closely at exactly what parts I'm missing. When my supervisor points it out, I just see that the whole thing isn't finished. I don't really know if it's scattered all over or just the end. I just know it's not done.\n\nDoctor: When you go back and look at one of these incomplete logs or paperwork that your supervisor pointed out, are the beginning and middle sections usually filled out completely, with just the final steps missing—or do you find that there are gaps scattered throughout different parts of the form?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know how to answer that any differently. I just haven't looked at them that closely. When my boss hands them back, I just see that I messed up again and didn't finish it. I don't really analyze where the blank spots are, I just know I have to fix it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But if it's something that takes a lot of focus for a while... it's really hard. \n\nLike, we have these certification modules and long procedure manuals we have to read, and I... I just put them off until the absolute last possible minute. Sitting there and trying to force my brain to absorb all those rules and steps, it honestly feels almost physically exhausting. I just try to avoid it as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did you have similar problems with finishing assignments, reading long materials, or staying focused on tasks that required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was actually an issue back then, too. My mom still has some of my old report cards, like from around fourth grade. They always said something like I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to actually finish my classwork and turn in my homework. \n\nI remember I would understand the lessons fine, but I'd just miss chunks of the instructions. Unless the teacher was standing right there keeping me on track, I'd just lose focus and not finish what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did you have particular trouble with reading, writing, or math specifically—like did you struggle to understand those subjects themselves—or was it more that you could understand the material but just had trouble staying focused and finishing the work?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble understanding the subjects themselves. Like I said, I usually understood the lessons just fine. It was never really about not getting the math or struggling to read the words. It was definitely more about just... staying focused long enough to actually get the work done. I just couldn't keep my attention on it to finish the assignments.\n\nDoctor: When you had to write things down in school—like essays, written answers on tests, or written homework assignments—did you find the actual writing process itself difficult, or was it mainly that you didn't finish writing what you were supposed to write?\n\nPatient: It was definitely just not finishing. The writing itself wasn't the problem. I could write fine, and I knew what I wanted to say, but... getting it all down on paper before I lost focus was the hard part. I'd just trail off or get distracted, and the essay or whatever just wouldn't get done.\n\nDoctor: Back in school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you read more slowly than other kids, or that you had trouble understanding what you were reading—even when you were able to stay focused on it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone ever said that. I could always read at a normal pace, and I understood the words perfectly fine. It was really just about keeping my attention on the page. If I could actually stay focused, I understood what I was reading without any problems.\n\nDoctor: Right now, in your daily life outside of work, when you're doing something that requires you to concentrate for a while—like reading a book, watching a movie, or having a longer conversation—do you find yourself losing focus or having your mind wander, or does that seem to work okay for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely happens outside of work too. I'll be trying to read or watch something, and my mind just drifts off. But honestly, it's the worst at work. I'm a meat processor, and even when I start a shift trying really hard to stay locked in, after a while... the cutting, weighing, and checking labels, it all just kind of blurs together. I'll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or log sheet for a while without actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. It just gets really hard to keep that concentration going.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did you ever get extra help, tutoring, or special services for reading, writing, or math—or did teachers ever suggest that you might have a learning disability in any of those areas?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't get any extra help like that. I never had tutors or anything, and no one ever brought up learning disabilities. The teachers just thought I wasn't trying hard enough or that I was daydreaming too much, but they never thought it was because I couldn't understand the material.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like a hobby, a game, or something you're genuinely excited about—do you still have trouble keeping your focus and finishing it, or does your attention seem to work better when you're doing something you like?\n\nPatient: No, if it's something I really like, my attention is actually fine. I don't really have trouble focusing on hobbies or things I'm interested in. It's just the boring or repetitive stuff where my mind completely wanders off.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those multi-step tasks at work, like the sanitation steps or batch logs, do you know what all the steps are supposed to be—do you understand the full sequence—but just lose focus before you get through them all? Or do you sometimes feel confused about what the correct order or steps are supposed to be in the first place?\n\nPatient: I know exactly what the steps are. I understand the whole sequence perfectly. The problem is just... I keep starting the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and somehow I just leave out the last parts. It's like I just lose focus before I get to the end, even though I know exactly what I'm supposed to do and I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. I just can't seem to follow through and complete the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: When you were a kid in school, did teachers or your parents ever say you were hyperactive, restless, or that you had trouble sitting still—or did you tend to fidget a lot or feel like you needed to move around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't the kid bouncing off the walls or anything. Teachers never said I was hyperactive or restless. I usually sat pretty quietly, honestly. It was more just... staring out the window or doodling instead of paying attention, rather than needing to run around or fidget all the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these multi-step tasks at work, is it specifically the last few steps that you tend to skip or leave incomplete—or do you sometimes lose focus and miss steps throughout the middle of the task as well?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I know I definitely leave off the last parts a lot, like I mentioned, but... I haven't really paid close attention to whether I'm skipping things right in the middle too. It's just that when the paperwork or the logs are checked, parts are incomplete. So I might be losing track halfway through as well, I just haven't noticed exactly where my brain starts dropping the steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing one of these multi-step tasks and you realize it's incomplete, do you typically find that you've skipped steps scattered throughout—like maybe missing something from the beginning, middle, and end—or do you mostly find that the beginning and middle parts are done but the final steps are left off?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm just not sure. Like I said, I haven't really looked that closely at exactly what parts I'm missing. When my supervisor points it out, I just see that the whole thing isn't finished. I don't really know if it's scattered all over or just the end. I just know it's not done.\n\nDoctor: When you go back and look at one of these incomplete logs or paperwork that your supervisor pointed out, are the beginning and middle sections usually filled out completely, with just the final steps missing—or do you find that there are gaps scattered throughout different parts of the form?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know how to answer that any differently. I just haven't looked at them that closely. When my boss hands them back, I just see that I messed up again and didn't finish it. I don't really analyze where the blank spots are, I just know I have to fix it.\n\nDoctor: The next time you get one of these forms back from your supervisor, could you take a moment to really look at it and notice: are the first parts and middle sections mostly filled in, with just the very last boxes or steps left blank? Or are there empty spots scattered all over the form in different places?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I can try to do that. I guess I usually just get so frustrated when I see it handed back that I just want to fix it as fast as possible and move on. But... yeah, next time it happens, I'll try to actually look at where the blank spots are and see if there's a pattern to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
